| 74 | 2026-03-14 | 4.69e-i-a-i-f-i-α-i-A-α-i-1-I-a `make repair-suppression-ref` as a CLI tool | 1 run | Full design of the surgical single-key suppression reference repair command. Core distinction vs. `make rename-surface`: repair is post-hoc (reference is already stale) and surgical (one field, one key), while rename is concurrent (you're doing the rename now) and broad (all affected files). The "surface not stale" guard distinguishes accidental surface reassignment from legitimate repair. Required args: `KEY` (must exist in string-surface-map.json) + `SURFACE` (must exist in budget.json). Optional flags: `--dry-run` (preview, no writes), `--validate-only` (feasibility check, exit 0/1), `--no-immediate-check` (skip post-repair condition eval), `--output-format=human|json|quiet`, `--no-stage` (write file but don't git add), `--confirm-surface` (when replacing a non-stale reference intentionally). Human output format: stale vs. new surface displayed with budget.json check, writing update line with before/after values, staging confirmation, post-repair condition evaluation ("current budget: 22 chars → condition NOT met, 7 chars above threshold"). Immediate-trigger case: post-repair evaluation shows condition currently met; command presents four options (A: accept and commit, B: adjust threshold, C: fork-string --dry-run, D: re-suppress); file is staged but terminal "waits to be read." Error cases: key_not_found with fuzzy key suggestions, surface_not_found with closest-match algorithm (same as audit), no_review_trigger, condition_no_surface, surface_not_stale, file_write_error, git_stage_error. JSON output: single flat object with status/key/stale_surface/new_surface/files_modified/staged/immediate_trigger/condition/suggested_commit_message; immediate_trigger: true produces status: "success_with_warning"; errors produce error_code from typed enum. Non-interactive CI mode: detected via TTY check + CI env variable + explicit --non-interactive flag; forced JSON output; immediate-trigger exits 2 (partial success needing follow-up) vs. human mode exit 0; decompose case exits 3 (human selection required, not just a warning). Decompose-in-CI: JSON error with error_code: "surface_decomposed" + decomposed_into array; CI creates PR comment asking developer to re-run with explicit SURFACE=<selected>. Comparison table covers 8 dimensions: semantic intent, timing, scope, primary input, side effects, surface-rename-log write, CI safety, idempotency. Dry-run output: identical structure to real run with [DRY RUN] prefix on each action line; real run feels conclusive by contrast. Three player journeys: Priya (quarterly audit finds 3 unevaluable conditions — confident match runs 10s cleanly; case-normalization match confirmed with --dry-run first; uncertain match traced through surface-rename-log.json before running; third repair triggers immediate-trigger warning, she forks using the provided command); Dev (PR blocked by Tier 1, copies repair command from error message, runs it, reads output, pushes, PR passes — never understood full l10n toolchain but solved the problem in under 2 min); Aarav (CI automation pipeline — `make l10n-suppression-audit --output-format=json` feeds unevaluable list, `--validate-only` pre-checks feasibility for multi-hop renames, `--output-format=json --non-interactive` applies repairs, exit-2 creates GitHub issue, exit-3 emits skip with human-needed label). Multi-hop chain discovery: when rename log has A→B and B→C, --auto-lookup should follow the chain; depth limit needed. Comparables: git cherry-pick vs. rebase (surgical vs. broad replay), kubectl patch vs. kubectl apply (targeted field vs. full declaration), npm dedupe (structural repair without rebuild), SQL UPDATE WHERE vs. schema migration. TikTok clip: developer runs audit, sees UNEVALUABLE rows, copies repair command from audit output, pastes twice, runs audit again — all green; "The audit tells you what's broken, the repair command fixes it, the CI confirms it worked." 5 new sub-aspects: repair_command field in audit JSON (I-a-i), --auto-lookup flag (I-a-ii), multi-hop rename chain detection (I-a-iii), batch repair rollback design (I-a-iv), repair command in IDE extension context (I-a-v). |
| 73 | 2026-03-14 | 4.69e-i-a-i-f-i-α-i-A-α-i-1-I Suppression condition reference integrity | 1 run | Core problem: `review_trigger.surface` is a string pointer into `surfaces.json`; surface renames silently break it; suppression stays active (not triggered) on unevaluable condition — correct behavior per parent analysis, but "silent" means nobody notices the broken pointer for months. Dual failure mode documented: (1) Silent false-inactive — renamed surface causes condition to be unevaluable while the new surface's budget actually meets the threshold; (2) Ghost trigger risk — treating unevaluable as triggered would cause cascade false firings on every surface rename, which is why the parent analysis correctly chose unevaluable=active+warn, but warn must be loud. Four sub-problems identified: Detection cadence, Detection visibility, Resolution path, Proactive propagation. 4 options analyzed: (A) Post-hoc detection only — nightly warning in build log, lowest visibility, 14-day fix window unenforceable in practice; (B) PR-time ref check on surfaces.json modifications (Tier 1, PR-blocking) — detects rename/delete/decompose in PR diff, fuzzy-match suggestion ("Did you mean `tooltip.workbench`?"), three rename patterns (simple/delete/decompose) require distinct suggested fixes, safety net for bypassed workflow; (C) Proactive rename propagation command `make rename-surface FROM=... TO=...` — atomic codemod: updates surfaces.json, regenerates budget.json, updates review_trigger.surface references in string-surface-map.json, scans review_trigger_note free text for mentions (highlights but does not auto-update), updates useSurface() call sites in component files, stages changes for review; decompose case requires interactive sub-surface selection; (D) Stable surface IDs — UUIDs as stable pointers, display names as mutable aliases; solves rename-induced staleness architecturally; costs: opaque code review, significant schema migration, doesn't solve decompose; overkill for current problem. Recommendation: Options B+C as complementary layers (C = proactive prevention when workflow is followed; B = safety net when it isn't; A = residual backstop). Three rename patterns taxonomy: simple rename (update to new name), delete (remove condition or change type), decompose 1→N (interactive sub-surface selection). Tier 1 error format designed: amber `❌ TIER 1 ERROR`, quoted key+condition, quoted old name, "was renamed in this PR → new name," copy-pasteable `make repair-suppression-ref` command, note about `make rename-surface` for future renames. Surface rename log (`l10n/surface-rename-log.json`) as versioned artifact pairing rename history for repair tooling. Immediate-trigger-on-repair edge case: after `make repair-suppression-ref` updates the surface reference, the command evaluates the condition against current budget.json; if immediately true, emits amber warning "condition is currently met — suppression will trigger on next nightly build." 3 developer journeys: Nadia (frontend dev doing taxonomy cleanup — runs `make rename-surface`, watches atomic propagation, fixes free-text note manually, PR passes cleanly; never knew about l10n suppression conditions before this); Priya (l10n maintainer quarterly audit — finds 2 unevaluable entries: `toast.legacy` renamed to `notification.toast` in October, `sidebar.Panel` → `sidebar.panel` case mismatch; closest-match suggestion correct for both; uses `make repair-suppression-ref` for surgical fixes; notes that Tier 1 PR check would have caught the bypass and files task to add to deploy pipeline); Dev (new developer — manually edits surfaces.json for hyphenation fix, bypasses command, gets Tier 1 error with clear copy-pasteable fix, learns `make rename-surface` from error message). | 72 | 2026-03-14 | 4.69e-i-a-i-f-i-α-i-A-α-i-1 Fork suppression expiry and review_trigger evaluation | 1 run | Formal lifecycle and condition expression language for fork advisory suppressions. 4 suppression states: active (true) / triggered ("triggered") / permanent ("permanent") / no suppression. "triggered" sentinel is key — distinct from false (never suppressed), signals that a condition fired and human response is required. 4 condition expression options analyzed: (A) Free-text — unevaluable by CI; purely documentation; guaranteed advisory debt accumulation; (B) Predefined condition types (RECOMMENDED Phase 1) — typed registry: budget_below {surface, threshold}, budget_ratio_exceeds {threshold}, surface_count_exceeds {count}, days_elapsed {days}, never {permanent_justification}; schema-validated; zero parser complexity; each is a simple function against static files; (C) Structured DSL with any/all logical operators — Phase 2; YAML-array form preferred over string DSL; enables composed conditions; (D) Two-tier: structured condition + optional review_trigger_note — recommended pattern; structured condition = CI-evaluable; note = human reasoning for future readers. Edge cases: (1) Condition already true at suppression creation — Tier 1 warns "condition is currently met, suppression will immediately trigger"; (2) Renamed surface reference — condition becomes unevaluable; suppression stays active (not triggered) to avoid false firings; repair warning emitted, must fix within 14 days. | 71 | 2026-03-14 | 4.69e-i-a-i-f-i-α-i-A-α-i Multi-surface string forking policy | 1 run | When to fork a multi-surface `shared.` key into two single-surface keys. 5 options analyzed: (1) Never-fork — always use surface overrides; works when English source is identical; fails when source itself diverges; (2) Always-fork — treat shared keys as temporary; fork on first override; too aggressive for most teams; (3) Budget-divergence-triggered — fork advisory fires when loosest:strictest budget ratio ≥ 1.67×; handles Scenario A (layout-driven budget divergence) but misses Scenario B (semantic divergence without budget gap); default 1.67 threshold is configurable in l10n/policy.json; (4) Semantic drift detection — nightly token overlap score between base key and surface variants, advisory at <40% overlap across 3+ locales; Phase 3 feature, too complex for initial implementation; morphological-variation false positives in Finnish/Turkish; (5) Manual forking with comprehensive tooling — RECOMMENDED: developer decides, `make fork-string KEY=...` command; dry-run shows files to create/update, call sites to rewrite, TM seeds; --apply creates 2-entry string-surface-map, rewrites call sites via codemod, emits l10n/tm-migrations.json with pre-seeding records. Option 4 (player-configurable — power-user flexibility, adds UI surface area, defaults still needed, most players won't touch it, wrong place for configurability); Option 5 (context-adaptive — marketing framing of Option 3 as "intelligence," unnecessary complexity); three attack vectors: (1) Direct Targeting — config built with coordinated stresses on buffer size, fallback filter depth, and priority queue depth simultaneously, generating the cluster signature without necessarily being the opponent's strongest build; (2) Asymmetric Coverage Attack — opponent's matches generate disproportionately high coverage in the cluster candidates vs. other opponents, so 5 matches vs. one opponent dominate a 45-match career analysis window; information asymmetry root cause: career analysis aggregates matches by default without visibility into which opponent caused which candidate entry — attacker shapes the sample the diagnostic is computed over, not the underlying truth; counter-design: Match-Source Breakdown section in agent audit panel — per-opponent bar chart showing each opponent's coverage contribution to the cluster; concentration warning fires when ≥60% of combined cluster coverage originates from one opponent (vs. expected uniform distribution ~5% per opponent in a 20-opponent pool); concentration ratio formula: opponent_contribution / expected_contribution — >5x = adversarial HIGH, >3x = adversarial MEDIUM, <2x = likely structural; adversarial root cause hypothesis promoted to #1 in root cause ranking when concentration flag is HIGH; "partial" result on Command Hub Overload scenario in Marcus journey prompts player to consider adding COMMAND-A to Paired Mode; three player journeys: Marcus 35 product manager first redesign — discovers isolation model, explores grayed-out agents tooltip ("locked during redesign"), runs Dense Formation stress test with coral-rendered signals, watches buffer thermometer turn red at third hop (problem visible in real time), increases buffer 80→120 and queue depth 3→5, reads diff ("I made RELAY-C bigger for the bigger job"), commits with version badge pulse; strengths: prevents destructive editing (staged model — discard always available), side-by-side simulation is uniquely powerful counterfactual affordance, isolation reduces cognitive load without hiding dependencies, Paired Mode handles coordinated redesigns that element-by-element fixing misses, templates lower floor without capping ceiling; weaknesses: stress tests from past failures can create false confidence (passing 3/3 known scenarios may miss untested scenario types), paired commit makes attribution harder (if one agent improved and one degraded, which to iterate on?), draft save needs context snapshot to rehydrate redesign intent across sessions, template homogenization risk if top-5 templates become de facto standard configs; | 58 | 2026-03-14 | 4.69e-i-a-vi-a-i Notification state in profile export | 1 run | Core question: should permanent suppress flags and active snooze timers travel with a profile export? Key insight: active snooze is time-bound (expires same calendar day), so it is almost always expired by import time — mechanically irrelevant to export; permanent suppress is the meaningful state since it represents the player's epistemic claim ("I understand this feature"); five design options analyzed: (A) Export Nothing — always reset on import, clean for community sharing, annoying for device migration where player has to re-suppress all learned notifications; (B) Export Permanent Suppress Only — correct for migration, problematic for sharing since community downloaders inherit "this player already knows what the toast means," suppressing reliability vocabulary introduction for new players; (C) Import-Time Prompt — presents suppressed notifications at import with per-item restore defaults; full player agency but usability problem (players click through dialogs without reading, button hierarchy does most of the work); (D) Export-Time Intent Label — disambiguation at point of maximum knowledge (player knows why they're exporting), but users still click defaults; (E) Export-with-Type-Field — Option D at implementation level; recommended: export permanent suppress only, never export active snooze, [Review →] opt-in drawer at import time; Phase 2 differentiates migration vs. config-share export flows so notification state is stripped from sharing at the system level rather than dialog level; Factorio per-save isolation, Slay the Spire run vs. persistent state, Steam per-account cloud save, shared ROM save as concrete failure mode of exporting suppress, Minecraft options.txt separation as comparables; Five-level control theory ladder taught through mission progression: Level 0 "The Manual Player" (pre-M6 frustration as pedagogical fuel), Level 1 "Bang-Bang Controller" (M6 — IF count < 2 THEN queue, succeeds because disturbance frequency < response frequency), Level 2 "The Oscillation Problem" (M7 — factory delay + repeated evaluation = integral windup → player invents anti-windup via `queue_count` condition from autocomplete discovery), Level 3 "Proportional Response" (M7-8 — multi-rule priority stack with emergency vs. attrition thresholds, step-function approximation of continuous P-control), Level 4 "Derivative Sensing" (M8-9 — `unit_destroyed_within(type, N_ticks)` condition as discrete derivative, tuning sensitivity via window width and threshold), Level 5 "The PID Equivalent" (M9-10 — four-rule stack combining emergency P-high, D-term trend, I-term with anti-windup, output clamp via `cancel_queue`). Mission design sequence: M6 calibrated for bang-bang success (confidence building), M7 breaks it with simultaneous losses (oscillation teaching), M8 escalating waves demand proportional response, M9-10 adaptive enemies require full PID. Key design decisions: `queue_count(type)` as discoverable autocomplete condition (not boot-log highlighted), `cancel_queue` as Mission 8+ action, production rules use system-level queries (no buffer cost) but temporal conditions (`destroyed_within`) consume 1 buffer slot. Interaction effects: Inspector "Control View" overlay rendering fire/idle waveforms, conveyor belt as visible actuator state, Sealed Watch as autonomous-pride moment, Doctrine constraints on thermostat complexity. Comparable: Screeps creep spawning (identical bang-bang → PID progression over 100+ hours compressed to 10 missions), Factorio circuit PID (powerful but untaught), Dwarf Fortress standing orders (bang-bang with hysteresis), real industrial PID tuning (1:1 mapping). TikTok clip: conveyor belt in overdrive during crisis, resource counter holding, unit count oscillating to stability — "I didn't build units, I built a thermostat that builds units." 4 player journeys (Tomás 16 discovering queue_count anti-windup through Inspector autopsy of oscillation failure, Dr. Suki 42 control engineer building MIMO controller that fails on sensor noise false positive from stun/destroy conflation, Rosa 62 retired teacher recognizing greenhouse thermostat metaphor and teaching grandson "dead band," Kwame 30 ML engineer streaming Gauntlet realizing his rule stack is a Kubernetes pod autoscaler). 5 new aspects discovered (3.19a-i-a through 3.19a-i-e: queue_count semantics, cancel_queue design, Control View Inspector overlay, MIMO resource contention, hooks-based vs system-level thermostat). |
